Q.6   [20

CO3 A   Explain, using quantum mechanical tunneling, why polonium emitting 8 MeV α-   [10
,   .   particles has a half-life of only hundreds of milliseconds, whereas uranium emitting   ]
BL-   4 MeV α-particles has a half-life of billions of years.
1, 2

Tunnelling probability with the nuclear barrier explanation and equations   - 4 M
Alpha decay is a natural example of quantum tunnelling in nuclear physics. An alpha particle
(helium nucleus) is confined inside the atomic nucleus by a strong nuclear attractive force
and a repulsive Coulomb potential barrier. Inside the barrier, the wave function does not
become zero but decreases
exponentially, and we do not observe the particle inside the barrier. The probability of finding
a particle is related to the square of its wave function, and so there is a small probability of
finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.
********** 8 **********

When two systems are entangled, the state of each composite system can only be
described with reference to the other state. If two states are not entangled, we say
that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

Q.7   [20
]
CO4 A Discuss what is meant by quantum entanglement between two systems? Illustrate the [10
,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.

CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark

finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.

,   quantum entanglement, explain how one can determine whether following two-qubit
BL-   state is entangled or not.
2, 3
When two systems are entangled, the state of each composite system can only be
described with reference to the other state. If two states are not entangled, we say
that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

Q.7   [20
]
CO4 A Discuss what is meant by quantum entanglement between two systems? Illustrate the [10
,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.
CO3 B. An α-particle of energy 6 MeV encounters a barrier of height 30 MeV and width   [6]
,   1.5×10−14 m.
BL-   Estimate the tunneling probability.
1, 2
Given:   1 M
E = 6MeV
U0 = 30 MeV
L = 1.5×10−14 m
Formula:   2 M
Calculations:   2 M
Final Answer:   1 M
T = 10-28
CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Circuit Diagram – 1 mark
Truth table – 2 marks
While Conrol and target qbits can be assigned in any order, the resulting output must
accurately reflect CNOT operation.
********** 12 **********

Tunnelling probability with the nuclear barrier explanation and equations   - 4 M
Alpha decay is a natural example of quantum tunnelling in nuclear physics. An alpha particle
(helium nucleus) is confined inside the atomic nucleus by a strong nuclear attractive force
and a repulsive Coulomb potential barrier. Inside the barrier, the wave function does not
become zero but decreases
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
exponentially, and we do not observe the particle inside the barrier. The probability of finding
a particle is related to the square of its wave function, and so there is a small probability of
finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.

BL-   state is entangled or not.

described with reference to the other state. If two states are not entangled, we say
that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

Q.7   [20
]
CO4 A Discuss what is meant by quantum entanglement between two systems? Illustrate the [10
,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.

,   1.5×10−14 m.

1, 2

E = 6MeV
U0 = 30 MeV
L = 1.5×10−14 m
Formula:   2 M
Calculations:   2 M
Final Answer:   1 M
T = 10-28
CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark

accurately reflect CNOT operation.

Tunnelling probability with the nuclear barrier explanation and equations   - 4 M
Alpha decay is a natural example of quantum tunnelling in nuclear physics. An alpha particle
(helium nucleus) is confined inside the atomic nucleus by a strong nuclear attractive force
and a repulsive Coulomb potential barrier. Inside the barrier, the wave function does not
become zero but decreases
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
exponentially, and we do not observe the particle inside the barrier. The probability of finding
a particle is related to the square of its wave function, and so there is a small probability of
finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.

described with reference to the other state. If two states are not entangled, we say
that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

Q.7   [20
]
CO4 A Discuss what is meant by quantum entanglement between two systems? Illustrate the [10
,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.

CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark

--- page 12 ---
Circuit Diagram – 1 mark
Truth table – 2 marks
While Conrol and target qbits can be assigned in any order, the resulting output must
accurately reflect CNOT operation.

,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.
CO3 B. An α-particle of energy 6 MeV encounters a barrier of height 30 MeV and width   [6]
,   1.5×10−14 m.
BL-   Estimate the tunneling probability.
1, 2
Given:   1 M
E = 6MeV
U0 = 30 MeV
L = 1.5×10−14 m
Formula:   2 M
Calculations:   2 M
Final Answer:   1 M
T = 10-28
CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Circuit Diagram – 1 mark
Truth table – 2 marks
While Conrol and target qbits can be assigned in any order, the resulting output must
accurately reflect CNOT operation.
********** 12 **********
````

Tunnelling probability with the nuclear barrier explanation and equations   - 4 M
Alpha decay is a natural example of quantum tunnelling in nuclear physics. An alpha particle
(helium nucleus) is confined inside the atomic nucleus by a strong nuclear attractive force
and a repulsive Coulomb potential barrier. Inside the barrier, the wave function does not
become zero but decreases
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
exponentially, and we do not observe the particle inside the barrier. The probability of finding
a particle is related to the square of its wave function, and so there is a small probability of
finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.

that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

Tunnelling probability with the nuclear barrier explanation and equations   - 4 M
Alpha decay is a natural example of quantum tunnelling in nuclear physics. An alpha particle
(helium nucleus) is confined inside the atomic nucleus by a strong nuclear attractive force
and a repulsive Coulomb potential barrier. Inside the barrier, the wave function does not
become zero but decreases
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
exponentially, and we do not observe the particle inside the barrier. The probability of finding
a particle is related to the square of its wave function, and so there is a small probability of
finding the particle outside the barrier, which implies that the particle can tunnel through the
barrier. This process is called barrier penetration or quantum mechanical tunnelling.

,   quantum entanglement, explain how one can determine whether following two-qubit
BL-   state is entangled or not.
2, 3
When two systems are entangled, the state of each composite system can only be
described with reference to the other state. If two states are not entangled, we say
that they are a product state or separable. One simple test that can be applied to
states is the following. This state is separable if and only if ad = bc

kr

where L is the width of the nuclear barrier.

Q.7   [20
]
CO4 A Discuss what is meant by quantum entanglement between two systems? Illustrate the [10
,   .   concept using a Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment based on photon and cat.   ]
BL-
1, 2
One of the most unusual and fascinating aspects of quantum mechanics is that
particles or systems can become entangled. For the simplest case of two quantum
systems, denoted as A and B, if these systems are entangled, it means that the values
of certain properties of system A are correlated with the values that those properties
will take for system B. These properties can remain correlated even when the two
systems are spatially separated, leading to what is often called spooky action at a
distance.   - 2 marks
Diagram – 2 marks
Explanation of diagram – 6 marks
In this experiment, a cat is confined in a box. A photon is incident on a 50–50 beam
splitter, which means the photon can take either path. Path A or path B with equal
probability. If the photon takes path A, it triggers a pistol and kills the cat, making it
dead. On the other hand, if path A is empty, that means the photon has taken path B.
In that case, the pistol cannot be triggered, and the cat remains alive.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
This situation can be described mathematically by a superposition state. If you do
not open the box, the cat is both alive and dead at the same time. This is the main
idea expressed by the equation. In physical terms, the cat has two possible states:
alive and dead. The same is true for the photon in path A; there can either be zero
photons or one photon. The important point is that the state of the cat and the state
of the photon are not independent of each other; they are connected. The cat is alive,
and the photon does not go through path A, or the cat is dead, and the photon goes
through path A. This means that the cat and the photon are entangled. An observer
who cannot see inside the box has no idea about the state of the cat or the state of
the photon. The observer also does not know whether the photon will go through the
path. After five minutes or even after a hundred years, because at any given time
there is only a probability that the photon has passed through path A. It may go
through path A, or it may not. Therefore, the observer can only describe the total
state of the system, which includes both the cat and the photon, as an entangled
state. Here, the cat and the photon are two subsystems. Together, the cat and the
photon form one total system. These subsystems are inseparably linked to each
other.

,   1.5×10−14 m.

1, 2
Given:   1 M
E = 6MeV
U0 = 30 MeV
L = 1.5×10−14 m
Formula:   2 M
Calculations:   2 M
Final Answer:   1 M
T = 10-28
CO4 C. Explain the working of the CNOT (Controlled-NOT) gate.   [4]
,   Determine the output states for all possible input combinations and present its truth
BL-   table.
2, 3
The two input qubits are called the "control" and "target" qubits. The control qubit
remains unchanged, but its state dictates what happens to the target qubit. If the
control qubit is in the state ∣1⟩, then an X gate is applied to that target; if the state of
the control qubit is ∣0⟩, then no change is made. In the notation below, assume the
qubit A (rightmost qubit) is the control, and qubit B (the leftmost qubit) is the target.
Below, the notation used is CNOT∣BA⟩.
- 1 mark
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---

Truth table – 2 marks

accurately reflect CNOT operation.
********** 12 **********

Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks

Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

Q.7
CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

CO-   D. Define qubit. List out the different types of qubit representations.   [5]
4;
BL-
Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.
---------3 marks
Numerical

1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.
---------3 marks

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks

Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

Q.7
CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

CO-   D. Define qubit. List out the different types of qubit representations.   [5]
4;
BL-
Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.
---------3 marks

The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)

Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks
********** 3 **********

Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.
---------3 marks

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented

CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks

Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

Q. 2
CO1, A. Discuss the experimental observations of the photoelectric effect and the failure of   [10]
BL-   classical electromagnetic theory in explaining it.
2, 3
The wavelength of maximum solar emission is observed to be
approximately 0.495μm. What is the surface temperature of the sun (assumed as
blackbody)?
Experimental Observations of Photoelectric Effect
1. Threshold Frequency ν0
No photoelectric emission occurs if the frequency of incident light is below a
certain minimum value, regardless of intensity.
2. Instantaneous Emission: Electrons are emitted without any time lag,
even at very low intensity.
3. Photoelectric Current ∝ Intensity: The number of emitted electrons
(current) increases with intensity of light (for ν> ν0).
4. Kinetic Energy depends on Frequency: Maximum kinetic energy of
photoelectrons increases with frequency, not with intensity.
5. Stopping Potential: A definite stopping potential exists to stop electrons;
it depends only on frequency.
-------   3 marks
Failure of Classical Electromagnetic Theory
Classical wave theory could not explain these observations:
1. No Concept of Threshold Frequency
Classical theory predicts that any frequency should cause emission
if intensity is high enough. Contradicted by experiment (threshold
frequency exists)
2. Energy depends on Intensity (Wrong Prediction)
According to classical theory, energy of electrons should increase
with intensity. But experimentally, kinetic energy depends on
frequency, not intensity.
3. Time Lag Expected
Energy absorption should be gradual → emission should take time
at low intensity.But emission is instantaneous
4. Continuous Energy Distribution
a. Classical theory assumes continuous energy transfer. But emission
shows discrete energy behavior.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.

Suppose there are N particles present in one-unit length along the x direction. If
each particle is moving with speed u, then in a small time interval ∆t, all the
particles present in a length u∆t will cross a fixed point. Therefore, the number of
particles crossing the point in time ∆t is N × u∆t Hence, the particle flux F, defined
as the number of particles crossing per unit time, is given by
Consider a group of quantum particles represented by a plane wave wavefunction
ψ(x) = Aeikx.
Such a wavefunction represents many identical particles moving in the same
direction with the same momentum. The quantity |A|2 represents the average
number of particles per unit length.

CO-   D. Define qubit. List out the different types of qubit representations.   [5]
4;
BL-
Definition: A qubit, or quantum bit, is the "fundamental information unit of
1, 2
quantum computing". While a classical bit can only be in one of two discrete states
(0 or 1), a qubit is a two-state quantum mechanical system that can exist in an infinite
number of states within a complex Hilbert space. Definition 2 marks
Qubit Representations
Qubits are represented through several mathematical and physical frameworks:
• Ket Notation (Dirac Notation): The state of a qubit is typically written as
|𝜓>. The standard basis states are |0> and |1>, which are the quantum analogs
of the classical 0 and 1.
• Vector Representation: In a two-dimensional complex vector space, qubits
are represented as column vectors:
o |0 > = (0 1)
o |1 > = (1 0)
• Linear Combination (Superposition): A general qubit state is represented
as |𝜓>= a|0 > +b|1 > , where a and b are complex numbers called
"probability". The sum of the squares of their absolute values must equal 1
(|a|2 + |b|2 = 1).
--------Representation 3 marks

discrete values of energy depending on the value of n. Thus. the energy becomes quantized due to
confinement of the particle. Hence, a particle in an infinite potential well can have only bound
states.

The scanning tunnelling microscope is a
practical application of quantum tunnelling
used to observe surfaces at the atomic scale.
A sharp metallic tip is brought within a
distance of about 1nm from a conducting
surface.
A small bias voltage V is applied between
the tip and the surface
Electrons tunnel through the vacuum barrier separating the tip and the surface.
The tunnelling current I depends exponentially on the tip-surface separation d:
where ϕ is the effective work function.
A change of 0.1nm in distance produces a large change in current, allowing atomic
resolution.
Key Physical Insights
The exponential nature of tunnelling explains why barrier width strongly affects
tunnelling probability.
Higher particle energy increases tunnelling probability.
Heavier particles have lower tunnelling probability due to larger mass m.
Tunnelling has no classical explanation and confirms the wave nature of matter.

Q.7
CO4, A. Explain the Bloch sphere representation of a qubit with appropriate diagram and   [10]
BL-   discuss the key aspects of quantum gates.
1, 2
The Bloch Sphere visualization, named after physicist Felix Bloch, is a geometrical
representation of a qubit’s state space. It is a unit sphere, which is a sphere with a
radius of 1. The vertical z-axis is labelled with |0⟩ at the top and |1⟩ at the bottom.
The ends of two perpendicular horizontal axes, the x and the y, are labelled |+⟩ and
|-⟩, and |i⟩ and |-i⟩, respectively. -----------2 marks
The z-axis represents the probability of the qubit being measured as a 0 or a 1.
The x-axis represents the real part of the state vector.
The y-axis represents the imaginary part of the state vector. ---------2 marks
A qubit is a combination of two states, |0⟩ and |1⟩. While in superposition, each has
a complex number coefficient. With α and  representing the coefficients, the qubit’s
state |ψ⟩ is written as:
|ψ⟩ = α|0⟩ + |1⟩   ------- (1)
The angles θ (polar angle) and ϕ (azimuthal angle) of the Bloch sphere are calculated
from these complex numbers. The angle θ is the angle between the state vector and
the z-axis, while the angle ϕ is the angle between the state vector counter-clockwise
from the positive x-axis. A third calculation is the length of the vector, which is 1
for pure states and less than 1 for mixed states.
With these three values, the previous equation for the qubit can be rewritten as:
-------- (2)
where  [0, ] and   [0, 2)
Above explanation with equation 1 and 2 --------4 marks
A Bloch sphere visualizes the relationship between a quantum state and two
important angles, θ and ϕ. In digital mode quantum computing, quantum states are
transformed by providing these angles of rotation.
